Requests appear immediately; completion waits for full background/result-delivery drain. Stopped single-click remains non-mutating, rapid second-click/Open launches through the existing gate, and running clicks reveal their editor. Profile default-mode selection never changes existing tabs. The [Sessions startup/work-status design](designs/2026-10-04-sessions-startup-and-work-status.md#implementation-evidence) records the accepted coordinated checks, package and installed proof and the earlier runtime observations: one paired first-leaf measurement of 10.673 → 2.853 seconds from recorded launch, and real asynchronous waiting with one completion only at true idle. These are bounded observations, not a latency distribution.

**Flat Sessions list, unread marker and selection pin.** `ompDesk.sessionsGrouping` (`flat`, the default, `folders` or `terminals`) is read by `SessionTreeProvider` through `SessionLauncherSource.grouping`; the layout picker only writes the setting (and the `ompDesk.sessionsLayout` context key picks which icon shows), and a configuration event refreshes the view, which then fires one root change. Both layouts are built from the same per-folder rows, so every row action, context value and state icon is unchanged. The flat list has one root-level row per session of the folders the grouped view would show (a row is listed once), labelled `folder · title` with folders of equal name widened by parent components (`flatFolderLabels`), and ends with a `New Session…` row whose command is plain `ompDesk.newSession` with no argument: the existing folder QuickPick (or Add Folder flow) and `newSession` path are used, not forked. A `Resume Session…` row (`history` icon) follows it and runs plain `ompDesk.resumeWorkspaceFolder` the same way; like the New Session row it is not a session, so `selectionRestore` puts the pinned selection back when it is clicked. Empty with no folder at all, so the view's welcome content shows. Order (`src/views/session-order.ts`, pure; 2026-10-13 amendment, before it rows were ordered by unread mark and last activity): rows whose process is not stopped (`stopped`/`draft` are the stopped group) come first and stopped rows follow; each group is alphabetical by folder label, then by session title (`Intl.Collator`, case-insensitive, numbers in numeric order), then older ordinal. The folders layout orders the rows inside each folder the same way; folder nodes keep their own order. A reorder is a root change; a presentation change repaints the row. **Unread** reuses the two identities the index already persisted: `lastCompletedReplyId` (catalog, shared) and `lastSeenReplyId`, both in the shared catalog row (an earlier design kept the second in the window's workspace state, which a window that opened another folder lost, marking every finished session unread). `TurnNotifier` reports every earned event (a finished, not aborted, turn or a question), notice sent or suppressed, through `onEarned`; the native ledger does the same through its suppression callback. `recordSessionEvent` mints a fresh event identity (`src/host/session-unread.ts`) and, when the user is looking at the session (`isLookedAt`: the editor in front, visible, in the focused window; the notification-suppression notion without the `ompDesk.desktopNotifications` switch), also marks it seen. `settleViewedSession` marks the front session seen on refresh, tab change and window focus. The row carries a `●` decoration badge (a `FileDecorationProvider` the provider exposes, keyed by an `omp-session:` `resourceUri`), `Unread · ` before its status and an `Unread reply` tooltip line in both layouts; a row a plain `omp` runs is never shown unread here, while a row another window holds shows the mark like its owner does, because every window derives it from the same shared markers; reading a session in any window clears the mark in all of them. **Selection pin** (`src/views/session-selection.ts`, pure): while a session has an open editor in this window, `pinnedSessionTab` (the most recently focused session editor of `EditorRecency` whose row is shown) is what `revealActiveSession` selects, and `view.onDidChangeSelection` puts it back (`selectionRestore`) when the selection holds no session row; picking another session row is left alone and the selection follows once that editor is viewed. Reveals keep `focus: false`. VS Code limits: a TreeItem label cannot be styled and a long label truncates the description (observed: the description vanished), so the mark is a decoration badge, which stays visible; and a pinned row inside a folder the user collapsed cannot be selected without expanding it, so the grouped view leaves that selection alone.

**Reply identities only advance across windows (2026-10-14 amendment to ADR-0034).** The shared session row is merged whole (`mergeSessionIndexRecords`), and a window that adopted another window's row did not then move its merge base (it does since the 2026-10-17 amendment below), so it later wrote that adopted copy back as if it had changed it. The unread marker used to be lost that way: after Reload All Windows a window that adopted the row before the user read it saved the row again with an unrelated change and replaced the newer `lastSeenReplyId`, while the window that runs the session (a live row, which `SessionIndex.refreshFromStore` leaves as it is) kept its own copy and, finding itself already read, never wrote again, so the row stayed Unread in every other window however often it was viewed. Both identities are now merged field by field with `laterReplyMarker` (`src/host/session-unread.ts`): the identities this module mints order by time and repeat count, and the later one wins whichever side holds it (an identity that cannot be ordered goes to the side that changed it since the base). `refreshFromStore` applies the same rule to rows the window runs, and writes the row back when its own identity is the later one, so a catalog that lost a read is repaired by the window that recorded it. Unread still means a reply earned while nobody looked; a row that is Working again after such a reply stays Unread until it is viewed, and viewing clears it from any window.

**A window's merge base follows what it adopts (2026-10-17 amendment to ADR-0034).** `SessionIndex.refreshFromStore` moves its `#sharedBase` to the committed value of every row it adopts, recomposes or sees removed, and to the committed bindings (which it mirrors whole); a row it leaves alone (a live host or a lifecycle operation in flight) keeps its old base entry, so that row's own change still reaches the merge and is reported. Before, a window's next write read every row another window had changed as its own edit: it reported a conflict whose two sides were identical (87 of the 87 retained session-row records of one real catalog, 84% of the document's size in all), and when the other window had committed again before this one adopted, the write took the older value back (a committed title was replaced by the one this window had adopted last). `mergeSessionIndexRecords` also reports no row conflict when the merged row equals the committed one, as the keyed merge already did, and the conflict ledger drops every record whose retained and rejected sides are equal on the next committing transaction; a record with a real disagreement is kept.

**Editor-slot bindings are dropped with their row (2026-10-17 amendment to [ADR-0025](decisions/0025-bind-editor-slots-to-current-conversations.md)).** `SessionIndex.remove` and the deleted-file removal drop the row's bindings in the same save; a window drops, in memory, the bindings whose row it does not hold at load and when a refresh adopts a record (a refresh saves it, a load leaves it to the next save). `bindEditorSlot` returns the committed binding without a write when conversation, role and refusal are unchanged and no rival needs demoting, and `setEditorMode` skips a mode already recorded, so a restored editor no longer commits the catalog twice on every restore. One controlling binding per conversation stays (broker provenance and a recorded stop read it). The passive binding of a closed editor is dropped by the window that saw it close (next paragraph).

**The passive binding of a closed editor is dropped by the window that held the editor (2026-10-19 amendment to [ADR-0025](decisions/0025-bind-editor-slots-to-current-conversations.md) and [ADR-0056](decisions/0056-publish-window-state-in-a-leased-registry-and-switch-windows-by-request.md)).** Every window publishes in its registry record `slots`, the slot ids of the editors VS Code holds in it (`BridgeHost.editorMembership`, restored tabs not yet shown included; absent = unknown, empty = none). `EditorSlotWatch` (`src/host/editor-slot-prune.ts`) remembers the slot ids this window has held in its own `workspaceState` (a reload keeps what an earlier host owed), and on each registry heartbeat `EditorSlotPruner.run` considers the ones absent for 30 s: it reads the other windows' records afresh and decides nothing while one is unreadable or lists no `slots`, forgets a slot another live window lists (moved there), waits for any window that has not published since the editor vanished, and otherwise calls `SessionIndex.dropClosedPassiveSlots`, which adopts the committed bindings and drops only a binding that is still passive, through the ordinary merged save (a binding another window changed since is kept). A binding of an editor this window never held, of a window that is gone (VS Code restores its editors with it) or of one that is opening an editor is never touched; a controlling binding stays until a later controller demotes it.

**Records of ended processes no window holds are settled once per activation (2026-10-17 amendment to [ADR-0034](decisions/0034-store-sessions-in-one-profile-catalog.md)).** Activation restores only the local cohort, so a row whose process ended while no window watched kept its recorded host and claim. `startRestore` (`src/wiring/catalog-adoption.ts`) now schedules `SessionIndex.sweepGoneRecords` 20 s after the restore settles (`GONE_RECORD_SWEEP_DELAY_MS`): for each row outside this window's cohort that records a host, whose claim is absent or held by a provably gone holder (`claimHolderProvablyGone`, `src/host/session-claim.ts`) and whose recorded process the reconciler proves gone, it takes the claim, clears the host with a conditional write (refused when the committed row records another process) and releases the claim; it never changes `runIntent`. It then deletes claim files whose holder is provably gone and which no row names (`listClaims`, `removeClaimOfGoneHolder`: re-read under the identity lock). A row another live window holds, launches or attaches is left alone because its claim holder is alive or the claim is taken first.

**Three Sessions layouts (2026-10-11 amendment).** `ompDesk.sessionsGrouping` is `flat`, `folders` or `terminals`. One title-bar button opens a QuickPick of the three layouts (`chooseSessionsLayout`, pure items `sessionsLayoutPickItems` in `src/views/session-order.ts`, the current one marked); the button is three commands (`ompDesk.chooseSessionsLayout.flat/.folders/.terminals`) that all open the same picker, shown one at a time by `ompDesk.sessionsLayout` so the icon shows the current layout, and `ompDesk.chooseSessionsLayout` is the palette entry. The context key `ompDesk.sessionsLayout` replaces `omp.sessionsFlat`. The `terminals` layout lists the background processes (see Sessions processes above). The Terminals group closes the flat and folders lists, after every session or folder and the New Session… and Resume Session… rows, set off by an inert blank row (`SpacerTreeItem`: no command, no context value; a TreeView has no separators); when nothing else is listed the group stands alone with no spacer. The title bar is Open Stats, the layout button, Stop Orphaned and Idle and Kill All.

New staged native hosts request-gate `nativeActivity({work:true})`; main/background work details come from one authenticated SDK state cut. Background work is the parent's async jobs and result deliveries **and** the live subagents of the OMP process: `hasLiveSubagents` (`src/omp/host-control.ts`) reads the process-global `AgentRegistry` through the injected SDK namespace (`pi.pi.AgentRegistry.global()`) and counts `sub` refs that are `running` (not an already accepted, no longer streaming run) or `idle` with messages queued for them; parked, aborted, advisor and main refs never count. A subagent revived from `parked` by an IRC message is in the registry but is neither a parent async job nor announced by a lifecycle frame, so without this a Terminal row showed Waiting for you and a Chat row settled while it ran. The Terminal path reads it inside `nativeActivity({work:true})`; a Chat (rpc-ui) child, which loads the same extension, answers the additive `subagentWork` control request (only to a child launched by a build that records the `subagents` capability), which the window polls every 3 s and merges through `withRegistrySubagentWork` into that tab's activity. Old hosts without that work detail remain Running unless idle is proved. Native end hooks strip async-specific claims: a fresh, non-aborted `willContinue` is provisional until authenticated SDK idle and every job/result delivery drain, and a new start/abort cancels it. A cancelled self-continuation that never starts is indistinguishable from an idle continuation gap; the maintainer explicitly accepted this hook-observation limit. Positive new-capability native background-work gating is covered by source/control-channel scenarios, not an installed long-running native case. Actual installed native detach/reattach was notification-silent; tagged WinRT history proves submission, not physical banner presentation. Same-artifact Chat and native/shell path-click receipts, restored fixture identities and retained shell-recovery/snapshot-link limits are recorded in the design.

Folder rows show the folder name followed by its cwd as a muted TreeItem description, middle-truncated to 40 characters while retaining root/drive and tail. Accessibility text includes the full path and session count. Session and folder tooltips are plain text with name, status and locale-relative last activity, plus one actionable line where needed. Last activity is the conversation's own: the `timestamp` of the last `message`, `custom_message`, `compaction` or `branch_summary` entry of the exact session file (`readSessionFileActivity`), read backwards from the tail only when an `fstat` on refresh shows the file changed; a folder shows its latest session's, and a fileless draft says No activity yet. File mtime is not used, because installed OMP also appends `session_exit`, model/thinking/service-tier and other bookkeeping entries and rewrites the title slot without a turn. The index's `lastActiveAt` is bookkeeping time (restore, reattach, conflict, failure, focus) that only recovers the focused tab; it keeps its persisted name for catalog compatibility and is never shown. The row context-menu **Copy Diagnostics** command copies the row's technical facts (including full paths, recorded identities and activity), using the existing capability redactor at the clipboard boundary.
Activity-signature transitions refresh Sessions directly, including dialog answer/cancel and work/wait changes; unchanged streaming text does not rebuild the tree. OMP queue facts stay count-only in Sessions; Chat lists the queued messages (see Queued messages). Background wait and continuation completion facts stay host-owned, not inferred from transcript wording.
The window-local launch/stop facts show **Starting/Stopping** while that operation is active. An unconfirmed attempt releases its lease and becomes saved/draft, never a stale-reservation state. Later actions probe current positive ownership anew.

**Sessions enrichment cost (2026-10-12; the heartbeat part replaced 2026-10-29).** `refresh({ ownership: true })` marks every row's ownership, header and conversation observation dirty; focus and showing the view call it. A header is re-read only when the file's `mtimeMs` or `size` changed since the last reading (`readSessionFileHeaderReading(file, previous)`, one `fstat` otherwise; a title OMP rewrites in place changes the time), as the conversation activity already was. Reads that complete between two enrichment turns repaint the view once: a completed read marks a projection due, and the `setImmediate` turn that dispatches the next reads (at most four of a kind) projects first and fires `onDidObserveConversation` once when any activity changed.

**Row ownership is observed again on events, not on every heartbeat (2026-10-29).** One observation of a row this window does not run is two claim reads, a holder-liveness check, the verified-writer reconcile (which can connect to the row's broker) and the outside-OMP lease probe; the registry's 15 s heartbeat used to repeat it for every row while Sessions was visible, in every window that showed it (12 rows: 48 observations a minute per window, measured by a test harness that counts `observeOwnership`). Now a row is observed again when something that decides its state changed: its claim file (`startClaimWatch`, debounced 150 ms, `refreshRowsForClaims` re-observes the rows filed under the changed claims), its catalog record (an adopted foreign commit changes the row's identity, `SessionTreeProvider.#identity`, which marks the row dirty; a commit that leaves the identity as it was costs nothing) and the set of live windows (`windowRegistryChanged` compares the other windows' holder ids with the last change and calls `refreshOwnershipForWindows`: the rows an ended window held, and every row not shown as held when a window opened; a change that only edits what a window shows, such as its folders or row statuses, does nothing). Marks are flags, so a burst of events costs one observation per row and an event during a flight one more, never one per event, and at most four run at once. The heartbeat's own work while the view is visible is `SessionTreeProvider.heartbeat(sweepAfterMs)`: each row's header and conversation `fstat`, an observation of the rows whose end no event announces (never observed or the last one failed; an outside OMP holding the lease; a writer under a broker no window drives; with 12 rows and two of them such, 8 observations a minute), and a **safety sweep** of every row when the last look at all of them is older than `ROW_SWEEP_MS` (3 minutes, twelve heartbeats; focus and showing the view restart the count). A hidden view does nothing on the heartbeat: events still re-observe its rows, and showing it observes all of them. Why 3 minutes: the watches are hints that a platform can drop, and every place a person looks (focus, showing the view) already looks at every row, so the sweep only covers a window that stays in front with the view open; a claim missed by its watch then shows within 3 minutes plus one observation, which is rare enough to cost nothing and short enough not to leave a row wrong for long. The earlier bound, 15 s for a missed event, is gone.

**Restart recovery and row actions.** Activation attaches, before any launch, each recorded `managed-rpc` slot this window is responsible for through the read-only authenticated probe, so a running child is adopted and never respawned; only a row whose run intent is `running` and whose old writer is proved absent is relaunched with `--session <exact file>`, and a stopped-intent row stays stopped and opens view-only. Tree rows use internal `ompDesk.clickSession` with an injected-clock recognizer; palette `ompDesk.openSession` launches using the former Resume semantics and the obsolete `omp.resumeSession` alias is removed. It reuses an already-open view-only panel or surviving bridged editor. The row context menu is state-specific (table above); plain `ompDesk.openSession` is no longer a menu entry because its default view is chosen through the two explicit entries. Close refuses before confirming when this window drives no transport for the row. See [ADR-0031](decisions/0031-reattach-a-surviving-managed-host-through-its-broker.md) and the [broker-aware restart recovery design](designs/2026-09-27-broker-aware-restart-recovery.md) for the surviving transport pinning.
A stopped session's explicit Resume request is allowed even though its editor holds no writer. It enters the same
claim-and-launch admission as the palette and row actions; passive editors still cannot mutate a running session.

**Row clicks carry no command argument.** A Sessions row's own click, `ompDesk.clickSession` for a session row and `ompDesk.openProcess` for a process row, is a tree item command **without** `arguments`; the handler takes the one row the view has selected (`SessionActions.clickSelected`, `openSelectedProcess`), because the workbench selects a row before it runs the row's command and sends the selection to the extension host ahead of the command on the same channel. The extension host replaces a command that has arguments by a delegate it registers for the tree node and disposes the moment the node is repainted, and the provider repaints a changed row in place (`fire(row)`, the clicked row included, at once, for its click feedback) while the workbench still shows the old row until a round trip later. A click that reaches the host in that gap, the second click of a double click, failed with the workbench's error `Actual command not found, wanted to execute ompDesk.clickSession /<n>` and was lost (a stopped row's second click is the launch). The empty folder's New Session row keeps its argument: it is repainted only with its parent, whose nodes the host disposes after the next refresh, not at the repaint.

**Resume from a stopped Chat.** The composer's Resume button shows in the `view-only` and `stopped` phases, and the host answers it in both: a history shown read-only, a session whose process exited (the live conversation object is still attached, in phase `stopped`) and one whose conversation was already released (the page keeps its last state). `ChatRuntime.handleMessage` raises `resume-requested` for each, and `resumeFromChat` (`extension.ts`) runs the row Open's path (`openTab` with the `resumed` verb → `index.restore` with `allowStoppedResume`), so ownership and claim checks, the one-writer rule and the exact-file launch are shared. Success replaces the conversation, so the page goes `stopped → starting → live` and its composer becomes writable. A refused or failed admission puts its reason on the page (banner; a conflict is also an error notification); a request that never reached the admission, or a Resume sent while the session is running or starting, is answered in the footer (`ChatRuntime.notify` / the fixed `CHAT_ALREADY_RUNNING_SENTENCE`). Nothing is silently ignored.

**Commands during a fresh editor's election.** A new editor is published passive until the index has durably recorded its control of the session, and its page can already be talking by then: the footer asks for the host's controls when it mounts, and a prompt may follow within seconds. Such a command is never dropped and never sent by an editor that does not control the session. The page messages that need authority (`omp:control-request` and the write commands of the chat vocabulary) wait, bounded to 15 s, for the election in flight on that slot (`dispatchSlotMessage`, `trackElection`, `awaitElection` in `extension.ts`) and are then routed on its outcome: delivered exactly once when the editor became the controller, otherwise refused visibly — the conversation tells the page the reason, and a control request is answered as unavailable with it. The role is published before the view mode is recorded, so recording the mode no longer extends the passive interval. Promotion asks the footer to read the host's controls again.

**Elections of one slot are serialized (2026-10-27).** `SessionIndex.bindEditorSlot` runs the elections of one editor slot one after another, in the order they were asked (`#slotElections`, a promise chain per slot). An election reads the session's claim and judges its holder before it commits, and judging a foreign holder waits for the process's native module and for a start-time reading, which can take seconds at window activation. A restored editor is bound twice in that window: when VS Code hands the tab over (`bindPanel`, which asks while the restore pass has not attached the session yet) and when the restore, an open or `electLocalController` attaches it. Run concurrently, the first election's refusal (the killed window still seemed to hold the session) committed after the second election's grant and replaced it, and the editor stayed read-only (*This editor is not controlling its session*) with nothing to elect it again. The regression test holds the native module's load and lets the second election finish first (`src/host/session-index.test.ts`, "lets the later election of one slot have the last word").

**Editor ownership.** VS Code owns open Webview panel membership and serializer revival; persisted identity is not a capability ([ADR-0019](decisions/0019-vscode-owns-omp-editor-persistence.md)). Closing a managed editor detaches without stopping its process. An editor is bound to one conversation for its slot; the tab is named for the session it shows (`sessionHeadline`, the same title the launcher row uses, plus the OMP icon) and corrected wherever a panel becomes a chat surface or Rename records a new stored title. Rename of a running session is rpc `set_session_name`, a stopped one rewrites its file. There is no native `/resume` or A→B switching in a chat process (`new_session`, `switch_session`, `branch` and `handoff` are not offered), so the slot-to-conversation binding changes only by the extension's own open/resume paths.

**Chat column.** With `ompDesk.chatColumn` on (default), a session editor created by `openPanel` (`src/wiring/editor-panels.ts`) targets the editor group that already holds a session editor (the active group if it holds one, else the leftmost) by its exact column; with none, it opens in a new group beside the active one, or the active group of an empty editor area. `src/host/chat-layout.ts` then waits for the panel's view-state report, moves a new group `column - 1` steps left and locks the group while the session editor is still active. `arrangeSessionPanelGroup` (`src/wiring/editor-panels.ts`) takes that column from the tab VS Code reports (`panel.viewColumn` keeps the requested column until a view-state event), because VS Code does not put an editor into an *empty* locked group even by exact column: after the last session editor of the chat group was closed (VS Code keeps the empty locked group), the reopened session lands in a new group on its right. It is then moved left from where it really is, and the emptied locked group, now on its right, is closed (`strayEmptyGroups`) instead of staying beside the chat. VS Code puts no editor into a locked group unless it is targeted by column (`ViewColumn.Active` included, verified on 1.141), so files from Chat links, the Explorer and Quick Open, Stats, Models/Agents, TODO/Agents details and folder shells open to its right without their own placement code. Restored editors keep the group and lock VS Code saved; the extension never re-locks a group the user unlocked except when it places another session editor there. With the setting off, session editors open in `ViewColumn.Active`.

**File window.** VS Code's Move Editor into New Window takes no arguments and opens a 1024x768 window (`createAuxiliaryEditorPart()` with no options; 1.141 remembers no size and has no setting for it). The first Ctrl+Shift+M of a group whose tabs are all *text* files (`TabInputText`, none untitled) therefore reopens the first file, with its selection, through the workbench's internal `_workbench.open` command, targeting `AUX_WINDOW_GROUP` (-3) with `auxiliary: { bounds }` (`FILE_WINDOW_SIZE`, 1600x1000; Chromium clamps a larger request to the screen); the public `vscode.open` drops the `auxiliary` option. The group's other files are then reopened into the new window's group in order (`vscode.open`, the active one last so it stays active) and the original tabs are closed; the file's document, so unsaved edits, stays shared. `workbench.action.enableWindowAlwaysOnTop` then acts on the focused, new window: the `alwaysOnTop` window option of the same call produced no topmost window on 1.141, and the command did, in the window's own title bar toggle and in the OS topmost flag. When any tab is not such a file (diff, viewer, custom editor, untitled file) the focused group is moved whole with `workbench.action.moveEditorGroupToNewWindow` (VS Code 1.141: `createAuxiliaryEditorPart()` then `mergeGroup` of the active group, no bounds, so a default-size window). Returning reopens each tab of the window's group by its input (`vscode.open`, `vscode.diff`, `vscode.openWith`) in the files group and closes it in the window, which VS Code closes once empty; a tab with no such input (untitled, webview, terminal) stays in the window, which stays remembered, and is reported. VS Code's `workbench.action.restoreEditorsToMainWindow` is not used: it merges the focused part's groups into the main part's active group (a no-op when run from the main window, the chat group when that is active) and touches every auxiliary window. Without a files group, the first tab is opened into the chat group by its exact column (VS Code accepts that for a locked group that holds editors; `ViewColumn.Beside` is relative to the focused window's active group, which is the file window's own when the key is pressed there, so it put the files back into the file window), which makes the main window's group active whichever window had the focus, and is then moved with `workbench.action.moveEditorToRightGroup` into a new group on the chat's right; the other tabs follow into that group. An auxiliary window's groups appear in `window.tabGroups` after the main window's, with ordinary view columns that shift when the main window gains or loses a group, so `src/host/file-window.ts` remembers the `TabGroup` object (one per group, its `viewColumn` kept current by VS Code) rather than a column; the window is gone when that object is no longer in `tabGroups.all`. `openTerminalFile` (Chat and Terminal file links) passes the file window's column to `vscode.open`; the Explorer and Quick Open cannot be redirected, but a locked chat group sends them to the most recently used unlocked group of any window, which is the file window once the main window has no file group of its own. The window is remembered by the extension host only: after a reload VS Code restores the window, but the next Ctrl+Shift+M opens another one.

Session naming uses the installed OMP title slot or session header, then the cached name, then a trimmed single-line first-user-prompt summary capped at about sixty characters, and finally the stable unnamed ordinal. An empty current title slot clears a legacy header name. Header identity reads remain prefix-bounded; an unnamed prompt beyond that prefix is found by streaming only through the first user entry. Imported/promoted titles feed the common Sessions, Processes and editor headline; authenticated live RPC name readback updates that cache and open detail titles without replacing their panels. User renames remain authoritative through OMP's own naming contract.
Reopening a still-running conversation gives its replacement editor a fresh immutable slot. When the current default matches the live mode, it retains the writer: moving retained state retires the old slot's terminal pipeline, the new panel binds a fresh frontend to the same native handle, native watches and run intent survive, and Chat resends its retained conversation. This also applies when startup restored the runtime before the closed editor was reopened. If the default differs, reopening uses the existing guarded mode replacement rather than bypassing shutdown, durable-content or one-writer checks. An unanswered terminal request disables stale input and reports response silence; only a lost established authenticated bridge connection produces the host-connection-lost message.
Before capturing transition facts, a switch joins the exact native runtime's already-pending authenticated-control attempt or the exact RPC session's memoized initial startup when it is still attaching/resyncing. This happens outside the lifecycle gate, which control publication itself needs. Completion revalidates editor/runtime/session/authority and the existing window-local open/view request sequence; a close, rebind or newer explicit selection supersedes the pending request, even when the newer choice keeps the current mode. No new retry is started, and genuinely unavailable or mismatched state still refuses replacement.

**Surviving-Webview bridge (implemented; installed acceptance observed for the rpc chat).** New editors use tab- and editor-specific view types and an identity-only persisted `{tabId, editorId}`; the serializer still restores editors after Reload Window. The host stamps such an editor's own `(tab, editor)` namespace into every document it builds, so the bounded *fallback* page a panel shows while it has no bridge endpoint saves the same version-2 state as the guest document and a page that never received the guest bundle cannot make its editor unrecognizable at the next restart. For an editor whose already-saved state is the version-1 `{tabId}` identity (an earlier build's fallback page, or a guest document with no bridge ticket), the serializer adopts it once — binding the editor exactly as a version-2 revival would, so its next document heals the state — only when the registered view type, the saved tab and the durable **controlling** binding all name the same `(tab, editor)` pair; a missing, passive, mismatched or foreign identity is still refused, and an unidentified editor persists nothing. A legacy generic editor is migrated lazily to one typed successor on first post-upgrade reload. `src/host/editor-coordinator.ts` elects one actual editor per indexed tab against VS Code TabGroups membership, not against stale index rows. `src/host/bridge-records.ts` and `src/host/bridge-endpoint.ts` provision a private per-editor document credential and an exclusive exact-port loopback listener (`src/host/bridge-listener.ts`), bound to the chat host's verified identity (owner generation, broker slot and generation, child pid and creation time, session id and file) before the secret is sent to the page. `src/webview/bridge.ts` and `src/webview/lib/bridge-client.ts` reconnect a surviving original document after Restart Extensions without replacing its chat client; the per-document route is acknowledged and fenced, while native mutations share an admission sequence across panel and bridge. A route's acknowledgement belongs to one wire connection: it is installed before readiness callbacks run and cleared when that connection ends, so a replacement connection's own acknowledgement triggers the authoritative snapshot resend. The new extension host reconstructs Sessions from actual editor membership and verified ownership. An orphan cannot be focused through a lost `WebviewPanel` handle; after a host-only restart the page names what stays unavailable until Reload Window (provider-account usage/settings refresh, provider login, `@` file completion, extension editor commands). Installed isolated windows (OMP 18.4.3) observed surviving pages dispatching and rendering prompts after repeated host-only restarts, including a session resumed from its view-only page, with model/thinking and Tools controls available. See [the reconnection design](designs/2026-09-25-reconnect-live-webviews-after-host-restart.md) and [ADR-0023](decisions/0023-reconnect-surviving-webviews-through-an-authenticated-bridge.md).

The rendered-document cache also keys on the embedded bridge document ID: upgrading a ticketless guest to a ticket-bearing document must actually reload its HTML. If the editor closes before its asynchronous endpoint bind finishes, its reservation is released synchronously and the late endpoint/records are forgotten; closing does not stop the native OMP process.

**Restored editors after a reload or workspace change.** VS Code revives a restored webview editor only when it is first shown, so after Reload Window every background tab exists in the tab strip but has no `WebviewPanel` in this window. `reviveEditorPanel` (`extension.ts`) is the one way to reach such an editor: it selects the tab through its group (`workbench.action.focus*EditorGroup`) and position (`workbench.action.openEditorAtIndex`), which makes VS Code hand the panel to the serializer, and waits (bounded) for `bindPanel` and then for the editor to finish taking its seat (`editorSettled`: controlling, not mid-open, not mid-transition), because Reload and mode switches refuse a passive editor and the election that records control follows the panel hand-over. A row click, the Open-in-Chat/Terminal commands, a second Open of a reserved row and Reload session all go through it, so none of them warns "already open" without showing the tab, and none needs the tab closed first; a page that survived an extension-host restart is only selected (its panel is never re-handed). A bridge record tree is filed under the workspace hash `W` that existed when the editor was rendered, and a surviving page keeps presenting that `W` and its port. The window's own hash follows its open folders and changes when a folder is opened or closed (an empty window becoming a workspace restarts the host and keeps the pages), so `BridgeRecords.homeWorkspace` looks an editor's own `(tab, editor)` directory up under every hash and takes the most recently written one; otherwise those pages find no document, bind a second port and show a lost connection. Activating a session's editor (tab switch, row click, Open) asks the next Sessions reveal to expand a collapsed folder and select the row (`requestSessionRowExpand`, `TreeView.reveal({select, focus: false, expand})`); other refreshes never undo a collapse. Notices shown to the user for these states are plain sentences, not component names.
**Which conversation a restored editor serves, and when its tab counts as shown.** An editor's view type names only the `(tab, editor)` namespace it was first opened under; after a settled switch the same editor serves another conversation. The activation pass (`syncBridgeEditors`), the serializer and the focus-composer lookup therefore all resolve the conversation the same way: the editor's reservation, else the conversation of its committed slot binding (when that row exists), else the view type's tab (`BridgeHost.editorConversation`). Reserving by the view type alone made two restored editors claim one conversation, and the real one was later closed as a duplicate. `selectEditorTab` (`src/host/editor-tab-selection.ts`) does not trust the result of `workbench.action.openEditorAtIndex`: VS Code 1.142's handler starts the open without awaiting it and does nothing while the focused group has no active editor pane. After focusing the group (its own command up to the eighth group, `focusNextGroup`/`focusPreviousGroup` stepping beyond it) and issuing the command, it waits until `tabGroups.onDidChangeTabs` reports the tab active, up to 2 s, and issues the command once more if it did not; only then is the tab reported as not shown. Each refusal reason (no tab, tab closed meanwhile, not active after the waits) is logged at info, and so is the "could not bring to the front" notice, which previously left no trace.
Explicit mode replacement/Reload renews the existing editor's document credential before successor launch; it never replaces the WebviewPanel. RPC commitment proves successor owner/broker/root/exact-file identity; native commitment uses process authority plus fresh available current-file/id facts. A host-only restart adopts the unchanged document/binding. Native terminal attach/input/resize/focus/visibility travel over the authenticated bridge and retain document/role/runtime generation gates; no credentials travel in frames.
On a surviving bridge page, Chat and its supported controls or the native terminal remain usable. A selector without a panel handle explains that Reload Window is required rather than creating a second tab. Panel-only completion/editor operations remain unavailable. Removed usage/settings/tool panels do not return. The no-model footer's **Log In to Provider** is a narrow application operation over the authenticated bridge: `provider-login` accepts only that fixed command after current document/guest-version/acknowledged-route checks and uses the indexed session's own profile. It may start the provider login flow (or its fallback terminal) from an eligible stopped Chat, but grants no model mutation, session writer or other Chat command and sends no credentials over the bridge. Passive editor login remains panel `postMessage` only: the existing passive bridge authentication/ACK prohibition is unchanged.
The native-footer follow-up also replays host-owned quota/Git metadata over the bridge; this is separate from the removed usage/settings panels described in earlier installed observations.

**Diagnostics.** `OMP Desk: Show Diagnostics` (`ompDesk.showDiagnostics`) opens an extension-owned read-only report regenerated per request under the `omp-diagnostics:` scheme: startup stages measured between real observation points with their provenance — OMP resolution, broker launch to child identity, first history paint, chat live, host control verified — plus the resolved OMP target (its reported version only when the runtime carries one), broker slot and child identity, host-control state, session and claim state, and extension/VS Code/platform/storage facts. A stage that was not observed renders `not observed` with its reason — never zero or an estimate — and secrets and capability-shaped text are redacted before anything is recorded. Prewarm remains deliberately unimplemented: no process starts ahead of time.

**Context, notifications and keyboard.** The context ring/popover shows host-reported tokens, context window and percentage, with unreported values unknown and no estimates. Optional metadata is host-owned, replayed to panel/bridge routes on attach, and separate from chat state. Session cost is the total OMP's own status line shows (`SessionManager.getUsageStatistics().cost`): `src/host/session-cost.ts` streams the bound session file and sums the usage of every assistant message, `task` result and `model_usage` entry, compacted history and abandoned branches included, as pi-coding-agent's `entryUsage` does. Installed OMP's `get_session_stats` only confirms the binding (its `cost` covers just the window after the latest compaction) and supplies the total for a session without a file. It is read on live attach and turn end with runtime/epoch/session-file/id fences; it is never reconstructed from the extension's bounded history or catalog pricing. OMP initializes absent/unpriced native totals to zero without an availability flag, so nonpositive, invalid or unavailable totals omit the cost line rather than assert a free session. Positive native totals display dollars to two decimals. Current-provider used quota windows come from installed `omp usage --json --redact --provider <provider>` at start/agent-end and effective-provider changes, with scoped five-minute coalescing/throttling and last-value retention on logged failures. The cache scope includes exact executable/prefix arguments, explicit profile and runtime cwd. The [compact-footer design](designs/2026-10-01-compact-chat-footer.md#quota-source-scope-and-account-rule) records the TUI-aligned model/tier precedence and documented first-report fallback when rpc-ui cannot attribute the active account; the context popover's quota tooltip shows all masked account windows/resets. Git uses the deepest containing `vscode.git` repository and state updates, falling back without a shell to bounded cwd `git rev-parse` calls; detached HEAD uses short SHA and outside-repo hides. Notifications still derive from the host conversation model (`turnActivity`), not a page report, only while its panel cannot be seen (`!panel.visible || !window.focused`). The first model output after a live snapshot is a baseline, so restart replay is not announced. Escape dismisses native model/thinking QuickPicks through VS Code; completion/context popovers retain independent owner state and one-press dismissal.

Quota popover rows show each native limit separately, for example `5h · 7% used · resets in 2h 14m`. Installed OMP's `UsageWindow.resetsAt` is an optional absolute epoch-millisecond timestamp; the existing usage parser carries it unchanged. The popover uses the local clock, refreshes countdowns every minute only while open, and keeps the native five-minute quota-cache policy unchanged. Positive remaining time rounds up to minutes and uses the largest two units (days/hours or hours/minutes). Missing timestamps omit the reset part; elapsed timestamps show `resets now / refreshing` until authoritative usage changes. Each reported reset also has its full local date/time in that row's title and accessible label; no reset is inferred from a `5h`/`7d` label.

**Provider usage status bar item** (`src/host/usage-status.ts`, `src/wiring/usage-status-wiring.ts`). One left-side status bar item (`$(pulse)`, id `omp.usageStatus`, so a right-click hides it) shows the same provider limits as the footer above, compactly: `5h 42% · 2h13m  │  7d 18% · 3d4h`. It is a second reader of the footer's data, not a second source: the text is built from the `windows` the active tab's footer binding already holds (`FooterUsage`, projected by `projectProviderUsage` from the scope's `ProviderUsageCache`), so there is no extra `omp usage` loop and nothing is derived from tokens. The source is `index.activeTabId` (the active Chat tab, else the one last active in this window); the item is hidden without a binding, provider, windows or anything to show. Rules, all in the vscode-free `usage-status.ts`: windows labelled `5h` then `7d` are shown; only when the provider reports neither, every projected window (`1d`, `monthly`) is shown in the same style, and no window is invented. Each window is `label percent% · countdown` and windows are joined by `  │  ` (U+2502 with two spaces on each side); a missing `usedPercent` omits the percent (`5h · 1h42m`) and a missing or passed `resetsAt` omits the countdown (`5h 19%`). The countdown is the time left, rounded up to whole minutes so it never reads 0 before the reset: it is written with the units `d`, `h` and `m`, joined without spaces, and a component appears only when it is above zero (`2d3h5m` for 2 d 3 h 5 min, `4h42m`, `52m`, `1d5m`, `1d`, `3h`); rounding up carries into hours and days (59 min 1 s is `1h`). A reset in the past shows no countdown (the percent stays, and the window no longer counts towards the background) until the next refresh replaces the reading. The background is `statusBarItem.warningBackground` from 80 % and `errorBackground` from 95 % of any shown window, measured on the percent shown. The tooltip lists the provider, every shown window with its exact local reset time, the account-selection note when present, and the reading's age (`ProviderUsageCache.readAt`, the time of the last successful read). The item refreshes whenever a footer binding is published or removed and whenever the active tab changes, and once a minute while visible (the timer runs only then and is cleared on hide and dispose). A click runs `ompDesk.refreshUsageStatus` (also in the Command Palette), which forces `refreshFooterMetadata` through the existing cache (`refresh(..., force)` skips the five-minute throttle; an in-flight read is joined). There is no setting.

**Runtime release and wire basis.** The extension resolves installed OMP, never bundles/pins it, and keeps protocol mismatch visible. Chat requires broker-negotiated RPC protocol 2 and exact guest protocol **6**, including required display-turn facts and normalized command metadata; no compatibility shim is supplied. Native entry/message parsers retain unknown content for reconciliation but never expose arbitrary internal details. `@oh-my-pi/pi-wire` supplies content types only. Frame decoding keeps the existing 64 MiB v2 chunk cap. Roster/TODO cuts apply synchronously at response dispatch. The VSIX retains licensed Codicon resources and `licenses/paseo/`; neither feature adds network permissions or host tools/MCP. Child history and display preferences use authenticated current-document bridge reads without conferring writer authority.

**Host-control pipe.** `src/omp/host-control.ts`, client/protocol and the in-process peer proof (`nativeControlPeerBridge`: `CreateFileW`, `GetNamedPipeServerProcessId` on that handle and the kernel creation time, then the handle becomes the socket through libuv's `uv_open_osfhandle`; [ADR-0076](decisions/0076-call-win32-in-process-through-a-staged-koffi-module-instead-of-starting-powershell.md)) authenticate either managed child. Protocol 3 keeps bounded binding/tool observations and adds public native state plus exact epoch/file/id-guarded shutdown/name actions. The signed shutdown acknowledgement precedes SDK commitment; ACK/EOF never prove exit. Native credentials are launch/process-bound and follow row rebinding; session file is readback, not native handshake authority. Model/thinking/Rename remain RPC in Chat; native Rename uses SDK name readback, while unsupported methods/control are honestly unavailable. File-evidence hooks remain consent-gated. OMP seals its process key to the pinned recipient; SecretStorage retains private recipient/proven key, with no secrets in environment, terminal or RPC ([ADR-0005](decisions/0005-preload-control-key-before-omp-startup.md), [ADR-0006](decisions/0006-host-generated-key-peer-verified-pipe.md)). Broker/PTY versions are unchanged; guest protocol 6 carries the presentation contract above.
New RPC-process control verification waits for `RpcSession.start()` to complete with live identity, not merely rpc-ui's early `ready` frame: the installed runtime handles `get_state` only after extension initialization has awaited `session_start`. Native control instead authenticates the exact process generation and obtains public SDK state when the TUI context becomes available; it does not impose an RPC startup barrier or bind its handshake to a mutable session file. Cwd, peer PID/process-generation and proven-key checks remain. Confirmed Close, Reload and recorded-host Stop release the old recipient, proven key and control-process record under the tab lifecycle gate; an uncertain stop retains them. Record/key publication uses that same gate and rechecks the captured runtime, so an old asynchronous completion cannot resurrect the stopped process's credentials or overwrite a successor's key. A reconnect to the same child uses its already-proven key.
**Host-control reattach.** A host process serves one control server per slot: OMP binds the `-e` extension again for every in-process agent session (each task subagent), so the module keeps a process-wide claim and later bindings stay inert; earlier builds started a second server (new key, new random-suffix pipe) per subagent that overwrote or, on stop, removed the slot's last-writer-wins rendezvous record, which after Reload Window made the extension refuse the record as `key-changed` or wait for a record that never returned although the original server was healthy. Extension-side reattach (`HostControlClient.connectWithProvenKey`) first dials every `omp-vscode-control-<slot>-*` pipe through the unchanged pid/creation-time peer proof and handshake and accepts the one whose server proves the key this launch pinned, so hosts launched by older builds recover after an in-place update plus reload without restarting OMP. Only when no server holds the pinned key is a changed record key accepted, and only as a rotation by the same process (record pid is the pinned pid, sealed to this launch's recipient, peer proof, host proves the new key); any other changed key stays refused ([ADR-0006 addendum](decisions/0006-host-generated-key-peer-verified-pipe.md)). A pinned key bounds the missing-record wait to three seconds, the failure reads as one line ("Host control lost … Reload the session to restore them."), and failed native reattaches back off from 5 s to 60 s.
Windows atomic replacement and its retry live in one module, `src/wire/atomic-file.ts`: `replaceFileAtomic` stages the content in a private `<target>.<pid>.<uuid>.tmp` sibling created exclusively (`stageBeside`, which the profile catalog's commit and the hard-link publishers of the runtime copies and the PTY records also use for their staging), renames it over the target and removes the staged entry on every exit; `retryFileOperation` retries only the transient Windows `EPERM`, `EBUSY` and `EACCES` sharing/access failures, retaining the staged file and existing identity lock and never unlinking the target. Two named budgets bound it: `RENAME_BACKOFF_MS` (nine attempts, waits summing to 2,070 ms) for the window registry, window requests and profile catalog, and `FILE_OPERATION_BACKOFF_MS` (seven attempts, 875 ms) for session claims, bridge records, the control rendezvous, native evidence, the native-file-observation store and OMP config, and for claim admission, which uses its first two waits (three attempts). Claim admission may retry transient failure before any launch; a restore that may already have started a writer is never replayed. Other errors and non-Windows failures propagate immediately.

**Staged runtime assets.** The helpers and the host-control module a child process loads run from content-addressed copies staged under extension global storage rather than from the installed extension folder (as does the broker tree), so a reinstall can rename the installed folder while a session runs; every copy is re-verified on every use (a copy whose bytes changed is staged again before it is handed to a child) ([ADR-0012](decisions/0012-run-child-process-entries-from-staged-copies.md)). Staging is not uniformly best-effort: where an asset is needed the stage is awaited and its failure surfaces (a host launch reports that the runtime entries could not be staged outside the installed folder), while the activation call is deliberately fire-and-forget so the helper path is configured before the process-generation probe used by the observation-history deletion proof, which re-awaits that same promise. The staged paths are not secret — they appear in the command line of the processes that load them — but the tree they live in is *verified*, not assumed: on Windows the access of the runtime directory, every digest directory, every copy, and every ancestor of the storage directory up to but excluding the OS-managed volume root must be limited to this account, SYSTEM, Administrators or TrustedInstaller — with the owner placeholders CREATOR OWNER, OWNER RIGHTS and SELF resolving to this account (the ancestor walk itself is derived by the shared private-storage convention from the layout root, not computed by the stager) — the copies and the directories that hold them with the strict rule, those carrier directories with the rule that no other principal may hold a right to replace the tree or change its permissions — a foreign principal that can only create or write inside them is tolerated, and so is a read-only shared ancestor — a directory this module owns that is not yet limited is rewritten non-recursively (a recursive `/T` rewrite leaves a file it reaches with no access entry at all), the owner of each of those paths is read and compared, and a symbolic link or reparse point is refused; on POSIX the mode bits and the owner count only while the listing shows no access marker, or GNU coreutils' context-only `.` (accepted on coreutils' documented meaning of that marker rather than on any check of the context itself): every path the store is reached through — the directories, every copy and the derived carrier walk — is read with the system's own listing, and the markers `+`, `@`, `%`, `?` or any unrecognised marker refuse the store, as does a listing whose mode cannot be read, so a macOS path carrying extended attributes or a Linux path with an ACL is refused rather than taken as proved. The cost is deliberate fail-closed rather than a narrow edge: on Apple's `ls` an attribute marker is printed instead of an ACL marker and accompanies the ordinary state of anything downloaded, tagged or unpacked, so such a chain makes the store unverifiable and the staged runtime unavailable on that machine. Anything unprovable refuses the stage, which is why a host launch can fail visibly on a machine whose storage directory is writable by another account. The volume root is the platform trust anchor: the walk stops there and the extension does not claim protection against a principal that controls it. This is protection against another *user* writing the staged code; arbitrary code running as this same user is outside ADR-0006's boundary and is not claimed against, and re-verifying a copy immediately before a spawn is a fresh reading rather than an atomic verify-then-execute. 

**Private Windows storage identity.** `src/wire/private-storage.ts` reads owner and access identities as security identifiers, and reads the current token's SID rather than parsing an account name. Since 2026-10-15 the reads are in-process calls through the staged `koffi.node` ([ADR-0076](decisions/0076-call-win32-in-process-through-a-staged-koffi-module-instead-of-starting-powershell.md)), not PowerShell: one batched call per verification pass (`readStorageSecurity` → `Win32Native.readSecurity`, `GetNamedSecurityInfoW` per path on the one worker-thread call lane under a 20 s deadline) reads the account SID (the process token's `TokenUser`) and, for every path the pass needs (store, its directories, verified files and every carrier), the owner SID and every ACE of the DACL (`src/wire/win32-security.ts` decodes the ACL bytes: access-allowed and access-denied ACEs with their numeric masks; a NULL DACL is one entry granting Everyone everything; an ACE type it does not know, a truncated ACL or an inconsistent size makes that path's access list unreadable). Each of the three reads still fails on its own channel (null access list, null owner, null SID), and a missed deadline, an error or a module that is not loaded leaves all three unread so every check refuses. `restrictPrivateStorage` runs the verification pass first (one call for a store that is already limited) and rewrites only when it refuses, deciding from that refusal's own reading (one fresh directory read when the pass stopped before reading), re-reading the remaining directories after each rewrite; when nothing was rewritten the refusal stands, and after a rewrite a closing pass from a fresh reading decides. When the whole reading failed there is no account SID and the rules are left untouched. Admission compares only SIDs; the account names (`LookupAccountSidW`, `namedPrincipal`) are looked up only for the principals a refusal message quotes and never grant trust. The non-recursive rewrite still runs `icacls` with numeric SID grants, with independent readback, so a rewrite starts one `icacls` process; a store that already meets the rule starts none. The staged tree the module itself is loaded from is verified before the host loads it by a one-shot child (`src/runtime/native-stage.ts`, bundled to `out/native-stage.js`, run by `runNativeStage` under the host's own executable as Node): it loads the packaged module, writes the staged copy and verifies the tree with the same native reads, and prints one JSON verdict; the host loads the staged copy only after a `staged` verdict, and a refusal or a missed 20 s bound leaves the module unavailable (every Windows check then reads as unknown and refuses). The staging child shows as one `tool spawn: node native module staging` line per host start. [ADR-0054](decisions/0054-verify-private-windows-storage-with-security-identifiers.md) records the original choice and its review status; ADR-0076 supersedes its mechanism.

**A refusal for another account's access is a class of its own (2026-10-29, [GitHub issue #1](https://github.com/hmmvot/omp-desk/issues/1)).** When the same-user boundary above refuses the staged runtime tree because another account can replace or re-permission a folder on the path to it (a `carrier`), can read the store itself, or owns a store path, the refusal is written by one module, `src/wire/foreign-access.ts`, so that the notice can say which folder, which account (SID and the name `LookupAccountSidW` gives it), what access (read, write, modify, full control or delete/permission-changing, from the access mask by the bit set `permissionsPermitReplacement` uses, `REPLACEMENT_FREE_MASK`), whether the entry is inherited, and which account runs VS Code: `…lets <SID> (<name>) replace or re-permission it (<mask>: <access>, inherited from a parent folder | set directly on it); this window runs as <SID> (<name>)`. The wording still begins as these refusals always did, the verdicts are unchanged (no check was relaxed), and the tail is read back from the string by `findForeignAccess` because the refusal reaches the notice only as text (the staging child's verdict, the loader's recorded failure, the log). `decodeAclEntries` reads `INHERITED_ACE` from each ACE's flags into `SecurityEntry.inherited`, which `parseWindowsAcl` passes on as an optional field. `classifyNativeFailure` reads this wording before the Windows messages and returns class `foreign-account-access` with the facts; the warm start's refusal (`chainSecurityProblem`, synchronous, SIDs only) is named by the loaded module before the load settles as unavailable (`nameForeignAccess`). The notice's **Open troubleshooting** opens the section for the class (`nativeTroubleshootingAnchor`), and **Copy diagnostics** adds `Folder`, `Other account`, `Its access`, `Entry` and `VS Code runs as` lines after `Error`.

**An account in the local Administrators group is inside the same-user boundary like the group (2026-10-30, [ADR-0054](decisions/0054-verify-private-windows-storage-with-security-identifiers.md) amendment, decided by the maintainer; [GitHub issue #1](https://github.com/hmmvot/omp-desk/issues/1)).** `isPermittedAclPrincipal(principal, currentSid, isAdministratorMember?)` in `src/wire/private-storage.ts` is the one classifier every check shares (the store and carrier entries and owners of the verifier and of the warm-start judge `chainSecurityProblem`, the executable trust rule `src/host/executable-trust.ts`, the restriction's "already limited" test). Besides the fixed SIDs and the account that runs VS Code it now admits a principal SID for which the read's `isAdministratorMember(sid)` answers `true`; the check functions take that function from the security read (`PrivateStorageSecurityRead.isAdministratorMember`, bound to the native module that made the read) or from the test probe, and a read that names none trusts nobody for membership. The module answers it with `Win32Native.administratorsMembership(sid)` (`src/wire/win32-native.ts`, `netapi32.dll` through the staged `koffi.node`): `LookupAccountSidW` names the group from `S-1-5-32-544`, `NetLocalGroupGetMembers` level 0 lists its direct members as SIDs (freed with `NetApiBufferFree`), the SID must be one of them and `LookupAccountSidW` must say it is a user account. Anything else is `not-member` or `unknown`, and both refuse, with the message an account that is not an administrator always got; a member reached only through a nested or domain group is not expanded and so refuses. `memoizeMembership` (`src/wire/win32-security.ts`) keeps each definite answer per SID for the life of the loaded module, never an `unknown`, so a change of membership is seen after Reload Window. The call is synchronous because the warm start judges access in one step, and is asked only for SIDs not already trusted that hold an entry that matters.

**Native provider login.** `OMP Desk: Log In to Provider…` (`ompDesk.loginProvider`) is available in the Command Palette, Sessions title overflow menu and the no-model Sessions row. With an active managed session it uses that exact executable, cwd and profile; without one it uses the default profile, or asks which profile when indexed sessions have another profile. It signs in through a dedicated short-lived `omp --mode rpc` process ([ADR-0070](decisions/0070-log-in-through-a-dedicated-short-lived-rpc-process-and-keep-the-terminal-login-as-the-explicit-fallback.md), [design](designs/2026-10-13-gui-provider-login.md)): a QuickPick of the providers OMP lists as available, a progress notification with Cancel, the sign-in address opened with `env.openExternal` and a pasted authorization code read through a masked InputBox that goes only to the child's stdin. API keys are not entered in VS Code: a provider that asks for anything other than a pasted authorization code, and the providers OMP's RPC refuses, end the login with a pointer to `omp` in a terminal and an **Open Terminal Login** action, which opens the visible `omp login` terminal that remains the explicit fallback (also the last picker item, **Other providers**). `OMP Desk: Log Out of Provider…` (`ompDesk.logoutProvider`, Command Palette and Sessions title overflow menu) removes a stored account after a modal confirmation. Chat's model picker always ends in **Log In to Provider…**, using that Chat's own scope, not another active editor. A successful session-profile `get_available_models` RPC read with no models changes the footer trigger to **Log In to Provider**, whose checked `omp:chat-command` opens the same native login path without selecting a model. Only the availability boolean is added to replayed footer metadata; catalogues stay host-local. Windows tools used by evidence and the control helper are resolved from `%SystemRoot%\\System32`, not a shadowable PATH. A terminal-only `/logout` typed into Chat is answered locally like `/login` and runs `ompDesk.logoutProvider` (palette scope: the active session's profile and folder); the Commands list's `/logout` runs the same flow in the profile and folder of the session that asked.

Every login and logout entry point shares one window-local guard (`providerAuthRun`): it is reserved before executable resolution and retained until the flow's OMP process has exited; repeated requests are answered, not queued, and closing the window ends the flow. An idle process ends by closing its stdin (OMP disposes its session and MCP connections; killed after 5 s); Cancel, a dismissed masked box and window disposal kill a login that is waiting on the user or browser, since OMP has no abort. The process starts the MCP servers the profile and folder configure (OMP 18.10.3 has no switch), so a kill is a process-tree kill (`taskkill /T /F`), issued before the plain kill. The pasted code and the sign-in address (only its origin is logged) never reach a log, notice, progress line or diagnostic; OMP's errors are classified into fixed sentences and OMP's own error text is never forwarded (ADR-0017). Text OMP sends for the user to act on (the masked box's prompt, `notify` progress, `instructions` such as a device code, and the account label of a log out) is shown, bounded and control-stripped, and never logged. A request while a fallback login terminal of this window is open is answered by showing that terminal (a same-named unrelated terminal does not release it); Chat's model picker, footer and the bridge `provider-login` request start the flow and are answered at once. A login or logout that sent its command, and a closed fallback login terminal, re-read this window's model catalogues and send every other live window of the profile a `models` request on the window-request channel (ADR-0056), so each re-reads its own without announcing it again; a window that does not answer is logged and reads the credential at its next availability read. Failed preflight releases the guard without dispatching. The page supplies no provider, profile, cwd or executable text; the host selects its indexed session scope, or the default profile when no session is available.

Model/thinking, running Rename and reads remain correlated by verified live RPC identity. Model availability is read once on live attach and after an effective model change or login-terminal close; successful picker reads also update it, and an automatic read that finds a turn running is deferred until the turn ends. Session catalogues use RPC only. Picker discovery works during a turn: OMP runs RPC commands in order and `get_available_models` waits for a provider refresh still in flight, so a Stop sent meanwhile can queue behind it. Thinking readback follows native events, not requests. The exact guest protocol keeps selections, metadata and presentation preference readback distinct; the Webview never receives pipe credentials or host configuration.

**Model and thinking choices at any time** (`RpcSession.chooseModel`, `chooseThinkingLevel`, `cycleModel`, `cycleThinkingLevel`; `ChatModel.pendingControl`, frame `control_pending`; 2026-10-11). Installed OMP 18.8.5 does not refuse `set_model`, `set_thinking_level` or the cycle commands while a turn streams (their handlers and the session code behind them have no streaming guard, while `fork`, `handoff` and `get_messages_page` do; the terminal UI cycles mid-turn too), and the agent loop reads the model and reasoning effort through getters per model request, so a mid-turn change applies to the next request. The former "OMP is busy; choose … after the turn ends" refusal was Desk's own guard on the catalogue reads. Choices are therefore sent at once. Only when the host must not write — a compaction pass (`get_state.isCompacting` or a running maintenance pass), a rewind in flight or fenced, the shutdown/view-change mutation fence, or a connection being restored — `RpcSession` holds the choice in `#held` (per kind, newest wins; unnamed "next" requests are counted, capped at 16, and an explicit choice replaces them) and publishes it to every page as the host-owned `control_pending` frame and snapshot field. The footer shows the held value with a clock icon, a dashed border and a tooltip; the readback stays OMP's own, and picking OMP's own value while another is held is a new choice that cancels it. Every transition that can lift the block (frame, phase change, fence, navigation end, snapshot) calls `#syncHeld`, which sends, in order, the model, its cycles, the thinking level and its cycles; a "not now" refusal keeps the choice held, anything else (OMP rejecting it, or no answer) drops it with a fixed notice, never retries it, and the footer returns to what OMP reports. `prompt`, `steer` and `follow_up` first wait for any held choice or in-flight application to reach OMP; a failed application refuses the message with `choice-failed` (the draft stays), and a choice that cannot be applied yet (compaction) refuses it as busy, so a message never runs on the old model silently. A held choice lives in the host session, so it survives a Webview reload; it is lost when the window or extension host restarts and when the session fails or stops.
The redundant persistent extension status bar is removed. Chat's authoritative model/thinking footer belongs to its own editor and uses model display names; Terminal already displays native model information. There is no global first-live-tab projection or permanent no-live-session item. Host-control commands remain available independently.

The authenticated channel retains a bounded names-only native tool catalogue for the independent clipboard command and the read-only Tools tree. Clipboard copy does not mutate selection. Live tool selection, cross-chat Paste and project startup presets remain absent under [ADR-0009](decisions/0009-read-only-native-tools-until-atomic-selection.md) and [ADR-0008](decisions/0008-native-tool-selection-at-session-start.md). A catalogue observation proves neither settled configuration nor approval/provider exposure. Language tools remain native OMP `lsp` only; the removed provider bridge is rejected history and separately gated future work ([bridge design](designs/2026-09-24-vscode-language-provider-bridge.md)). OMP's own capability providers still own context files, skills, plugins/extensions, MCP servers and agents. The user-requested read-only session inventory narrowly amends the former no-inventory boundary without porting Claude resources, scanning directories or starting a second OMP process.

**Skills, Tools, MCP and Extensions views** (`src/host/inventory/`, `src/wiring/inventory-wiring.ts`, `src/views/inventory-tree.ts`, `src/omp/inventory-worker.ts`, `src/omp/config-transaction.ts`; [design](designs/2026-10-11-skills-tools-mcp-extensions-views.md), [ADR-0061](decisions/0061-read-the-omp-inventory-through-a-staged-bun-worker-and-write-omps-own-toggle-state.md), both accepted after independent architecture review). Four tree views share one model and one tree provider. Each has a Session | Global scope: Session reads the active or last-focused editor's folder and profile and overlays what that session reports (skill commands, `dumpTools`, authenticated `listTools`, name-matched MCP tools; descriptions are plain text, absence is claimed only from authenticated evidence); Global reads a project-free folder and the worker refuses a polluted one. The inventory comes from a staged, digest-verified Bun worker (`out/inventory-worker.mjs`, same spawn/receipt helper as the Models/Agents worker) that imports the installed OMP's own dashboard inventory, cross-checks it against warning-bearing capability loads and OMP's effective skill loader, and returns a bounded redacted projection; a failed or unreadable inventory falls back to the session's own report with no switches. Checkboxes switch items globally only, by writing OMP's own user-level state (`disabledExtensions`, the built-in `<tool>.enabled` table in `src/omp/inventory-switches.ts`, the user MCP config's `disabledServers`/`enabledServers`, the plugin lock) through the shared config transaction extracted from ADR-0048; project files are never written and YAML comments are not preserved. Each switch is bound to the folder, profile and scope of the list the row came from, serialized, and reported with OMP's own application behaviour ("may apply live" or "restart a running session"). Reads happen only while a view is visible and results of an older selection are discarded. These views never change a running session's tool selection (ADR-0009).

**Inventory precedence and failure reasons (2026-10-12 amendment to the paragraph above; [design](designs/2026-10-11-skills-tools-mcp-extensions-views.md), [ADR-0061](decisions/0061-read-the-omp-inventory-through-a-staged-bun-worker-and-write-omps-own-toggle-state.md) §9–10).** An MCP name is one row owned by the first entry in OMP's loader order (provider priority, then file order), with contributing files and what each does in its tooltip (`src/omp/inventory-mcp.ts`); a lower same-name entry is shown as shadowed by the owner's file, and a server OMP's dashboard drops for an identical connection is a `shadowed` row whose `shadowedBy` names the survivor (`isSameMCPConnection` from the installed OMP; dashboard computation, which can differ from a session's when the earlier server is switched off by name). `InventorySnapshot.incompleteReasons` carries, per incomplete view, a fixed-template line for each failed source (`src/omp/inventory-reasons.ts`: provider name and local file for known shapes, otherwise a category; never OMP's raw warning payload), bounded and re-validated in `inventory-runtime.ts` (300 characters, 20 per view with an overflow marker); the banner shows two, the OMP Desk log every retained one.

**Context view** (`ompDesk.context`, `src/host/context/`, `src/wiring/context-wiring.ts`, `src/views/context-tree.ts`; [design](designs/2026-10-12-context-view.md), [ADR-0063](decisions/0063-read-the-context-breakdown-from-omps-own-context-report-and-files-from-the-system-prompt.md), accepted after independent architecture review): a tree for the focused or last-focused Chat session (the inventory views' selection). While visible it subscribes to that session's outputs for the live header (model, usage — the Chat footer's source — maintenance) and runs a read-only deep read (`RpcSession.readContext`: `get_state`, `get_session_stats`, a scan of the session file for cost, compactions and clear boundaries) at attach, on a changed selection, on Refresh and after settles, never during a compaction or a pass and never on a timer; a running turn does not stop the read at attach or after the selection's generation changed (otherwise one long turn leaves only the live groups), and a read the session could not answer is retried at its next phase change or snapshot. The category breakdown is OMP's `/context` report, requested only by Read Breakdown (`RpcSession.requestContextReport`, a normal prompt whose command output stays in the Chat; the request is a fenced operation: nothing else is sent while it runs). Context files are extracted from the system prompt text. Compact and Shake act on the viewed tab through the Chat's own flow.

**Settings view** (`ompDesk.deskSettings`, `src/host/desk-settings/`, `src/protocol/desk-settings.ts`, `src/webview/desk-settings/`; [design](designs/2026-10-12-settings-view.md), [ADR-0062](decisions/0062-render-the-settings-view-from-the-contributed-schema-in-a-webview-view.md), accepted after independent architecture review): a Webview view, last in the OMP Desk container, listing every `ompDesk.*` setting generated from this extension's own `contributes.configuration` (`context.extension.packageJSON`). `src/protocol/desk-settings.ts` projects each schema entry to a control (boolean toggle, enum dropdown, number/string/array input) and fails closed to Open in Settings editor for shapes it cannot edit; `controller.ts` is the only writer: it validates the value against the schema, writes the chosen configuration layer (User, or Workspace only for window-scoped settings), serializes writes per window, answers each request with a correlated result and re-reads the effective value, source layer and overrides on `onDidChangeConfiguration`. Reset clears the User and Workspace values (disclosed on the button). `page-state.ts` is the pure reducer: drafts keep unsaved text across live updates, own writes are attributed by request id, a draft left while its save is unanswered is deferred until the answer. The page uses native labelled controls, `aria-describedby`, `aria-live` results and a nonce CSP with no remote content. Changing `ompDesk.sessionsGrouping` interacts with the Sessions picker warning in `src/extension.ts`.

Native `tool_call` and `tool_result` hooks now produce bounded file observations for supported local `write`/`edit` paths after explicit workspace/session consent, verified private storage and disclosure of pre-approval capture. Records and blobs are checked on every read, bound to the native owner/slot/process/session and shown in extension-owned read-only editor documents and VS Code diffs; the Webview receives no raw bytes. Admission observations are not immediate before-images, and missing results remain unknown.
